=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a directed graph with 2N vertices and 2N-1 edges.
The vertices are numbered 1, 2, \ldots, 2N, and the i-th edge is a directed edge from vertex i to vertex i+1.
You are given a length-2N string S = S_1 S_2 \ldots S_{2N} consisting of N Ws and N Bs.
Vertex i is colored white if S_i is W, and black if S_i is B.
You will perform the following series of operations:

- Partition the 2N vertices into N pairs, each consisting of one white vertex and one black vertex.
- For each pair, add a directed edge from the white vertex to the black vertex.

Print the number, modulo 998244353, of ways to partition the vertices into N pairs such that the final graph is strongly connected.
 Notes on strongly connectedness
A directed graph is strongly connected if and only if it is possible to travel from any vertex to any vertex by following edges.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
S

Output

Print the number, modulo 998244353, of ways to partition the vertices into N pairs so that the final graph is strongly connected.

Constraints


- 1 \le N \le 2\times 10^5
- S is a length 2N string consisting of N Ws and N Bs.
- N is an integer.

Sample Input 1

2
BWBW

Sample Output 1

1

Vertices 2,4 are white, and vertices 1,3 are black.
Let (u,v) denote an edge from vertex u to vertex v.
If we pair up vertices as (2,1), (4,3), the final graph have the edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (2,1), (4,3). In this case, for example, it is impossible to travel from vertex 3 to vertex 1 by following edges, so this graph is not strongly connected.
If we pair up vertices as (2,3), (4,1), the final graph have the edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (2,3), (4,1). This graph is strongly connected.
Therefore, there is exactly 1 way to pair up the vertices that satisfies the condition.

Sample Input 2

4
BWWBWBWB

Sample Output 2

0

No matter how you pair up the vertices, you cannot satisfy the condition.

Sample Input 3

9
BWWBWBBBWWBWBBWWBW

Sample Output 3

240792

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

This means the prefix interval [1,k] has more whites than blacks; the excess whites are "exported" to the right. This is like a Dyck path where W>B.

Maybe strong connectivity requires that for every prefix with W_k >= B_k, at least one prefix black is matched to a suffix white? So condition can be stated: For every k, if B_k <= W_k, then at least one of the B_k prefix blacks is matched to a white >k. This is a "noncrossing" condition on the matching: no prefix with surplus whites can have all its blacks matched internally.

This is reminiscent of "parking functions" or "Lagrange inversion": Count permutations with no "deficient" prefix? Let's define a matching as a permutation between black and white positions. If N appears at position p (1<=p<=d), then:
- For i < p, M_i is max of first i elements from {1..N-1}; constraints A_i < N need to hold.
- At i=p, M_p=N >= A_p.
- For i>p, automatic.
The elements before p form a permutation of some p-1 values from {1..N-1}; the elements after p are the remaining N-p values in any order. The constraints before p depend only on the relative order/values of those p-1 elements, but the values are not necessarily {1..p-1}; they are a subset of {1..N-1}. The running maximum among them is their actual maximum. Condition M_i >= A_i for i<p. This is similar but with a set of available values excluding N, and thresholds A_i < N. The actual values matter, not just ranks, because thresholds are absolute.

Process descending t=N..1:
State r = R_{t+1} (min position among values >t), initially ∞. We need choose σ_t unused such that min(r, σ_t) <= f_t.

Actually f_t nondecreasing in t, so as t decreases, f_t decreases or same. Thus L_t (unused positions <= f_t) may vary.

If r > f_t, then we must choose σ_t <= f_t. Number of choices = L_t. After choosing, new r = σ_t <= f_t.
If r <= f_t, then any unused position works: t choices. After choosing:
- If σ_t <= f_t, new r = σ_t (could be smaller).
- If σ_t > f_t, new r = r (since r <= f_t < σ_t).
So r remains <= f_t. For next step t-1, threshold f_{t-1} <= f_t. If r <= f_{t-1}, then again unrestricted; if r > f_{t-1}, then forced. Thus the only relevant information is whether r is <= f_{t-1} for the next threshold. More generally, r is the minimum unused? Actually r is min position among placed values (values >t). Since we process descending, placed values are the larger white indices. r is the smallest black index used by any larger white.

We can think of choosing positions for values N,N-1,...,1. The condition R_t <= f_t means that when we have placed values t..N, their minimum position is <= f_t. Since f_t nondecreasing, this is equivalent to: For each t, the smallest position used by values t..N is no larger than f_t. This is a "deadline" for the smallest position among a suffix of values.

Let's try to derive a product formula using the process but with a smarter state.

Process descending t. At step t, threshold f_t. Let's define k = f_t. The condition requires that among values t..N, at least one is placed in positions 1..k. When processing from N down, this is equivalent to: before placing value t, if no value >t has been placed in positions 1..k, then we must place value t in positions 1..k. If some value >t already in positions 1..k, then value t can be anywhere.

This is exactly the classic "parking process" for permutations with "preferences" maybe. Let's define for each prefix of positions [1..k], the first value (largest? actually from right) placed there. The condition says for each t, by the time we have placed values t..N, the prefix [1..f_t] is nonempty. Since f_t nondecreasing, as t decreases, the required prefix shrinks? Wait f_t nondecreasing with t, so for smaller t, f_t <= previous, so required prefix is smaller or equal. Thus the condition becomes easier as t decreases? Actually for t=N, need prefix [1..f_N] nonempty among values {N}; so value N must be in first f_N positions. For t=N-1, need prefix [1..f_{N-1}] nonempty among values {N-1,N}; since f_{N-1} <= f_N, this is a smaller prefix, but includes more values. It may already be satisfied by value N if σ_N <= f_{N-1}; otherwise need value N-1 in that prefix. This is like scanning values from high to low and maintaining the leftmost filled position.

Let's define h = the smallest position among values >t (r). At step t, if h <= f_t, condition satisfied; else need place t in [1..f_t]. Since f_t is nonincreasing as t decreases, if h > f_t, we place t at some position <= f_t, which becomes new h (<=f_t). If h <= f_t, we may place t anywhere; if we place it at a position < h, h decreases, which could help future (since future f smaller). So there is a choice to decrease h voluntarily by placing current value in a small unused position. This voluntary decrease affects count but maybe can be accounted by "any unused position" choices.

This process is similar to counting permutations with "record minima" and can be solved by considering the set of positions that are "safe" (<= current f). Let's attempt to compute total count via dynamic programming over h but maybe h only takes values among f_t and positions. Can we find formula for general N using r constraints? Let's try to count permutations with M_b >= r_{b+1}. This is a problem of permutations with lower bounds on prefix maxima. There might be a known formula:
Number of permutations π of [N] such that π_i <= a_i is ∏ (a_i - i +1). For prefix maxima lower bounds, maybe use inclusion-exclusion or "rook polynomial" on a Ferrers board.

Let's express constraints as avoiding empty rectangles in permutation matrix. For each b, M_b < r_{b+1} means all first b entries are < r_{b+1}, i.e., the rectangle rows? Let's use π as permutation matrix with columns positions (black index), rows values (white index). Constraint M_b >= r_{b+1} means in the first b columns, there is at least one row >= r_{b+1}. Equivalently, the rectangle columns 1..b, rows r_{b+1}..N is not empty. Since r_{b+1} nondecreasing, these rectangles are nested? As b increases, columns expand, rows shrink (r increases). The forbidden event is empty rectangle. We need all these rectangles nonempty. This is a "Ferrers board" condition: For each b, the permutation has a point in the "northwest"?? Actually columns 1..b (left) and rows >= r_{b+1} (top). Since rows are white indices increasing with position; larger row = later white. This is top-left? Let's draw columns left to right, rows bottom to top (row=white index). Need each prefix of columns intersect suffix of rows.

This is equivalent to the permutation having no "cut" where the first b columns use only rows < r_{b+1}. Since there are b columns and r_{b+1}-1 rows below threshold. If b > r_{b+1}-1, impossible (automatic). If b <= r_{b+1}-1, the condition says the set of rows used by first b columns is not contained in the bottom r_{b+1}-1 rows.

This is exactly the condition that the permutation is "indecomposable" with respect to a skew shape? Maybe can be counted by the "transfer-matrix" or "rook polynomial" on a Ferrers board. The number of permutations with at least one point in each of certain rectangles can be computed by inclusion-exclusion over b:
Valid = ∑_{T⊆[1..N-1]} (-1)^{|T|} #perms where for all b in T, M_b < r_{b+1}.
If constraints for multiple b hold, what does that mean? M_b < r_{b+1} for b in T. Since M_b nondecreasing and r_{b+1} nondecreasing. This is equivalent to M_b <= r_{b+1}-1 for b in T. The set of constraints may be nested. Counting permutations with upper bounds on prefix maxima M_b <= u_b for some b. Is there a simple formula?

Let's consider a single constraint M_b < r. This means the first b entries are all < r. Number = choose/order? There are r-1 values < r. Choose b of them and arrange in first b positions: P(r-1, b) = (r-1)!/(r-1-b)!, and arrange remaining N-b values in last positions: (N-b)!. So count = (r-1)!/(r-1-b)! * (N-b)! if r-1>=b else 0.

For multiple constraints M_b < r_{b+1} for b in a set B. Since M_b nondecreasing, if M_b < r_{b+1}, then for b'<b, M_{b'} <= M_b < r_{b+1}, but their thresholds r_{b'+1} may be smaller. The constraints are not simply nested. However, the event that M_b <= u_b for all b in B can be counted by considering the maximum value among first b. Maybe equivalent to a single constraint at the largest b? Let's see.

Suppose we require M_{b1} < u1 and M_{b2} < u2 with b1<b2. Since M_{b2} >= M_{b1}. If u2 >= u1, then M_{b2}<u2 doesn't imply M_{b1}<u1. If u2 <= u1, then M_{b2}<u2 implies M_{b1}<=M_{b2}<u2<=u1, so the b1 constraint is redundant. Here u_b = r_{b+1}-1. r_{b+1} nondecreasing, so u_b nondecreasing with b. Thus for b1<b2, u1 <= u2. The b2 constraint has larger upper bound, so it does NOT imply b1. The b1 constraint is stronger (smaller upper bound) but on a shorter prefix. So multiple constraints matter.

Counting permutations with M_b <= u_b for a nondecreasing sequence u_b (where u_b = r_{b+1}-1) might be doable. This is the complement of our condition. We need count permutations where for all b, M_b >= u_b+1. Let's call valid. Maybe count invalid via inclusion-exclusion over the first violation.

Let's define the first b where M_b < u_b+1. Since M_b nondecreasing and u_b nondecreasing. Let b0 be the smallest b with M_b < u_b+1. For b<b0, M_b >= u_b+1. At b0, M_{b0} <= u_{b0}. Since M_b nondecreasing, for all b>=b0, M_b <= M_{b0}? Wait M_b nondecreasing, so M_b >= M_{b0}; could exceed u_b later. So violation at b0 doesn't persist. But the first violation might allow recursive counting.

Maybe use the "position of maximum" recursion. Let's revisit.

Let A_b = r_{b+1} for b=1..N-1, nondecreasing, A_b <= N. We need M_b >= A_b.

Let pos_N = position of value N. Since M_b = N for b >= pos_N. Thus constraints for b >= pos_N are automatically satisfied. For b < pos_N, M_b < N, so need M_b >= A_b. Since A_b < N for b < pos_N? After placing N at position p, the prefix before p (positions <p) contains p-1 values from {1..N-1}; constraints for b < p require their prefix maxima >= A_b. Since A_b < N. The values are {1..N-1} with the same order. Thus the number of ways to fill the first p-1 positions satisfying constraints for b=1..p-1 is exactly the number of valid permutations of length p-1? But in the original, before p, we have p-1 values from {1..N-1}; their maximum can be up to N-1, not just p-1. So it's not a permutation of length p-1; it's a subset of size p-1 from N-1 values. The thresholds can be larger than p-1. Need count of partial permutations (injective sequences) of length L=p-1 from [N-1] with prefix maxima constraints. This suggests a more general counting problem: count injective sequences of length L from [N] with prefix maxima lower bounds.

Maybe there is a formula for injective sequences with prefix maxima constraints. Let's generalize.

We have a set of available values [N]. We choose an ordered sequence π_1..π_L of distinct values (L<=N). Let M_b = max_{i<=b} π_i. Count sequences satisfying M_b >= A_b for b=1..L, where A_b nondecreasing. This is like placing L rooks in an N x L board (columns positions, rows values) with constraints that each prefix of columns has a rook in rows >= A_b. The remaining N-L values are unused. Counting such partial permutations.

Maybe this can be counted by a product using the following: Process columns left to right. At column b, before choosing π_b, we have used b-1 values. Let m = M_{b-1}. Need after choosing, M_b >= A_b. If m >= A_b, any unused value works. If m < A_b, we must choose π_b >= A_b. The number of unused values >= A_b depends on how many such values already used. But since m < A_b, no used value >= A_b. Thus all values >= A_b are unused! Great. So if current maximum m < A_b, then the number of choices for π_b to satisfy is N - A_b +1 (all values from A_b to N are unused). If m >= A_b, any unused value works: N - (b-1) choices. After choosing, m updates.

This is much simpler! The state m only matters relative to A_b. Since A_b nondecreasing. At step b, if m < A_b, we are forced to pick a value >= A_b, and all such values are unused. Number of choices = N - A_b +1. If m >= A_b, we can pick any unused value: N-b+1 choices.

We can process columns and maintain m. But maybe we can derive a product formula by considering intervals where A_b is constant.

Let's sort distinct thresholds. Let A_b = r_{b+1}. Nondecreasing. Suppose thresholds take values v_1 < v_2 < ... < v_k. For b in an interval where A_b = v, the requirement is M_b >= v. If m >= v', no forced at next interval; if m < v', forced at first column of next interval.

Let's formalize for the full permutation length N (we place all values). We process b=1..N. For b=N, A_N? There is no constraint for b=N (since constraints up to N-1). We can set A_N=1 or N? Actually after placing all values, M_N=N. We can ignore. But the process with N columns and constraints for b=1..N-1; at b=N, no constraint, choose remaining value.

Let's define A_b for b=1..N-1, and A_N = 1 (no constraint) maybe.

We need count permutations π length N. Process b=1..N:
- Before choosing π_b, m = max used (m=0 initially).
- If b<N and m < A_b: forced to choose π_b >= A_b. Number of choices = N - A_b +1 (since all >=A_b unused).
- Else: any unused value, choices = N - b +1.
After choosing, update m.

This process depends on m only through whether m < A_b. Since A_b nondecreasing, we can think of thresholds increasing. Let's define the "current maximum" m. Initially 0. At each step b, if m < A_b, we must "jump" to a value >= A_b. The number of choices for the jump is N - A_b +1, but among these, some are < A_{b+1} (if A_{b+1} > A_b) and some >= A_{b+1}. If m >= A_b, no jump; but m might still be < A_{b+1} later.

This is similar to a Markov chain with state being the interval [A_b, A_{b+1}-1] etc. Since N up to 2e5, we can do DP over thresholds with O(N) if we aggregate.

Let's define distinct threshold values. Let A_b for b=1..N-1. Since A_b nondecreasing, it is a step function. Let positions where A_b increases. Suppose at position b, threshold v. We can process intervals of constant v.

Within an interval of length L where A_b = v:
- At the first position of the interval, if current m < v, forced jump to >=v. After that m >= v.
- For the remaining L-1 positions, since A_b = v and m >= v, no forced; any unused value works. The maximum m may increase if we choose a value >m, but it remains >=v.
At the end of the interval, m is some value >= v (if interval nonempty and we survived). Then next threshold v' > v. If m >= v', then at next interval first position no forced; if m < v', forced.

But note within an interval, if m >= v at the start, we have no forced for all L positions. We can choose any unused values, and m may increase. We need track distribution of m after the interval, specifically whether m >= v' for next threshold. Since v' is the next threshold. Values range v..N. If m < v', then m ∈ [v, v'-1]; if m >= v', then m ∈ [v', N]. The number of ways to end in these categories after filling L positions with arbitrary unused values can be computed combinatorially.

Maybe there is an even simpler product formula. Let's test with the process for full permutations and thresholds A_b.

Consider the forced jumps. At a forced step with threshold v, we choose a value >=v. If we choose a value >= next threshold v', then this single choice may satisfy multiple future thresholds. If we choose a value in [v, v'-1], then at next threshold v' we will be forced again (unless there are non-forced positions in between where m could increase above v'? Wait if after forced at v, m ∈ [v, v'-1]. Then for remaining positions in current interval (A=v), no forced, but we might choose a value >=v' during those positions, increasing m above v'. If so, by the time next interval starts, m may be >=v'. So the state after an interval is not just determined by the forced choice; arbitrary choices in the interval can also increase m. Thus we need account for all choices in the interval.

Maybe we can count using the "record maximum" sequence. The constraints M_b >= A_b mean that the running maximum must reach each threshold v by certain deadlines (the first b with A_b >= v). This is exactly the R_t <= f_t condition but from the π side. The process with m is natural.

Let's attempt to derive a product formula by considering the positions of record maxima. The running maximum m_b starts at 0. It increases at some positions (record values). The constraints require that by the time b reaches d_v = min {b | A_b >= v}, the running maximum must be at least v. Let d_v = f_v? Actually f_v = min b with r_{b+1} >= v = min b with A_b >= v. So constraint M_{d_v} >= v. Since M_b nondecreasing, this is equivalent to M_b >= v for all b>=d_v. Thus for each value v, the running maximum must reach v by position d_v. This is a deadline for records.

Now, the running maximum reaches v exactly when the first value >= v appears. That is R_v (same as before). So R_v <= d_v. This is the R_t constraint with f_t = d_t. Good.

Counting permutations by record deadlines: We have values 1..N. Each value v has a deadline d_v (nondecreasing) by which some value >=v must have appeared. Equivalently, the first record value >=v must appear by d_v. Since records are increasing values. Let record values be v_1 < v_2 < ... < v_k=N, appearing at positions p_1<...<p_k. The condition R_v <= d_v means for each v, the first record value >=v appears by d_v. If v is between record values v_{i-1}+1..v_i, then R_v = p_i (position of record v_i). Need p_i <= d_v for all v in that range. The hardest is v = v_i (largest v in range) because d_v nondecreasing, so d_{v_i} is largest; wait need p_i <= d_v. If d_v is larger for larger v, then the hardest (smallest d_v) in the range is v = v_{i-1}+1. But if p_i <= d_{v_{i-1}+1}, then it holds for all larger v. So condition for record i: p_i <= min_{v = v_{i-1}+1 .. v_i} d_v = d_{v_{i-1}+1} (since d nondecreasing, min at lower end). Let e_i = d_{v_{i-1}+1} (with v_0=0). This is the deadline for the i-th record value (the first record >= previous+1). Since d_v is nondecreasing, e_i is nondecreasing? As i increases, lower end increases, so e_i nondecreasing.

Thus constraints on record positions: p_i <= e_i for each record i. But p_i are increasing positions. This is like scheduling records with deadlines.

Maybe count permutations by record values and positions. There is a known formula: The number of permutations with a given set of record values and positions is 1 for records, and arbitrary for non-records. Summing over record sets satisfying deadlines might yield a product.

Let's attempt to count using the "record process" from left to right. At each position b, we choose a value. The running maximum m. If m < A_b, forced to choose a new record >=A_b. If m >= A_b, we can choose either a new record >m (which will be a record) or a non-record <m. This is exactly the process. Maybe we can compute the total count with a DP over m but optimize using the fact that A_b nondecreasing.

Let's define dp[m] after processing some prefix = number of ways with current maximum exactly m. Initially dp[0]=1 before any positions. At position b with threshold A_b:
- If m >= A_b: we can choose any unused value. Number of choices depends on m and b: unused values total N-(b-1). Among them, values >m become new maximum; values <m keep m. So transitions:
   dp_new[m] += dp[m] * (#unused values < m)  (choose non-record)
   for m' > m: dp_new[m'] += dp[m] * 1? Actually choosing a specific unused value >m: there are N-m unused values >m? Wait values >m are all unused because m is current maximum, so no used value >m. Thus number of choices to set new max m' is 1 for each m' in (m,N] that is unused? All >m unused, so for each m'>m, one choice. So dp_new[m'] += dp[m].
- If m < A_b: forced to choose value >=A_b. Values >=A_b are all unused (since m<A_b). Choices:
   For m' = m (cannot, because choose >=A_b >m) no.
   For m' in [A_b, N]: dp_new[m'] += dp[m] * 1 (choose m').
So transitions are simple: from any m, you can either stay at m by choosing a non-record value <m (if allowed), or jump to any higher value m' with coefficient 1 (if choosing that value as new maximum). The number of non-record choices <m is m-1 - (b-1 - #records?) Wait used values <m count? Since current maximum m, all used values are <=m, and at least one equals m. The number of used values <m is (b-1) - 1? Actually if m was just reached as a record at current or previous position, there is exactly one used value equal to m (the record). All other b-2? Let's be careful: At the start of position b, we have processed b-1 values. Current maximum m. If m=0 initially. If m>0, the value m has been used exactly once (unless m=0). All used values are <=m. Thus used values <m = (b-1) - 1 = b-2 if m>0. For b=1, m=0. So the number of unused values <m is (m-1) - (b-2) = m - b +1. This matches the flat-step choice count earlier. If m=0, no non-record.

Thus transition:
At position b (1-indexed), before choosing, if m >= A_b (or b=N no constraint):
- stay at m: dp[m] * (m - b +1) choices? For m>0. For m=0 at b=1, m-b+1=0, ok.
- jump to m'>m: dp[m] * 1 for each m'>m.
If m < A_b:
- jump to m' >= A_b: dp[m] * 1 for each m' >= A_b. (No stay because m'=m not allowed; also m'<A_b not allowed.)
This DP is over m=0..N. Naively O(N^2). But transitions are range additions: from each m, add dp[m] to all m'>max(m, A_b-1?) Let's formulate.

At each position b, new dp'[m'] = 
- contribution from staying: dp[m'] * (m' - b +1) if m' >= A_b (or no constraint) and m'>0.
- contribution from jumping: sum_{m < m'} dp[m] where m >= A_b? Wait if m >= A_b, jump to any m'>m. If m < A_b, jump to any m' >= A_b (which implies m'>m since m<A_b<=m'). In both cases, from any m, you can jump to any m' > m, but if m < A_b, m' must be >= A_b; since m' > m and m<A_b, m' could be between m+1 and A_b-1, which are <A_b and not allowed. So allowed m' > max(m, A_b-1). More precisely, if m < A_b, allowed m' >= A_b. If m >= A_b, allowed m' > m. This is m' > m and m' >= A_b. Since if m>=A_b, m' > m >=A_b, so m'>=A_b automatically. Thus unified: from m, jump to any m' > m with m' >= A_b. Since m' > m and m' >= A_b. If m >= A_b, m' > m implies m'>=A_b. If m < A_b, m' >= A_b. So allowed m' >= max(m+1, A_b). Also m'<=N.
So jump contribution to dp'[m'] = sum_{m < m', m >=? actually m can be any <m' and if m < A_b then m' >= A_b automatically; but the condition m' >= max(m+1, A_b) is equivalent to m < m' and (m >= A_b or m' >= A_b). If m' < A_b, then need m >= A_b? impossible m<m'<A_b, so no jump to m'<A_b. Thus dp'[m'] for m' < A_b has no jump, only stay if m'>=A_b? no stay requires m'>=A_b, so dp'[m']=0 for m'<A_b. This makes sense: if threshold A_b > m', current max cannot be < threshold after position b.

So for each position b:
Let T = A_b (for b<N), and for b=N, T=1 (no constraint).
dp_new[m'] = 
- stay: dp[m'] * (m' - b +1) if m' >= T and m'>0 else 0.
- jump: sum_{m < m', m >=?} dp[m] for m' >= T? Actually if m' >= T, jump from any m < m' is allowed? If m < T, m' >= T so allowed. If m >= T, m' > m so allowed. Thus for m' >= T, jump contribution = prefix_sum_dp[m'-1] (sum dp[m] for m=0..m'-1). For m' < T, jump contribution = 0.
But wait if m' >= T, from m=0? m=0 < T, allowed jump to m'>=T, yes choosing first value. Good.

Thus DP update:
For m' from 0..N:
if m' < T: dp_new[m'] = 0 (except maybe m'=0? At b=1, T=A1>=1, dp_new[0]=0; correct because after first position max>=1).
if m' >= T:
dp_new[m'] = dp[m'] * (m' - b +1) + sum_{m=0}^{m'-1} dp[m].
Here dp[m'] is old dp at same m. Note if m'=0, stay factor negative; but m'>=T>=1 so ok. If b=1, stay factor m'-0? m'-b+1 = m'. For m'>=T, dp[m'] old is 0 except dp[0], so dp_new[m'] = dp[0] for m'>=T, i.e., first value can be any >=T. Good.

This DP is O(N^2) if done naively, but the update has a nice form: dp_new[m'] = dp[m']*(m'-b+1) + prefix_sum[m'-1], for m'>=T, and 0 below T.

We need compute after N steps, total valid permutations = sum_m dp[m] after N (should equal count). N up to 2e5; we need optimize this DP.

Observe T = A_b = r_{b+1} is nondecreasing. The update zeroes out all states m' < T at each step. Since T nondecreasing, the set of possible m after step b is m >= T (and m >= b because max of b values >=b). Actually dp[m]=0 for m < max(b, T). The recurrence might be simplified.

Let's compute dp after each step. Let S_b[m] = number of partial permutations of length b with max exactly m and satisfying constraints up to b. Recurrence:
For b=1..N:
T_b = A_b for b<N, T_N=1.
dp_b[m] = 0 for m < T_b.
For m >= T_b:
dp_b[m] = dp_{b-1}[m]*(m - b +1) + ∑_{x=0}^{m-1} dp_{b-1}[x].

Let P_{b-1}[m] = ∑_{x=0}^{m} dp_{b-1}[x] (prefix sum). Then jump term = P_{b-1}[m-1].
So dp_b[m] = dp_{b-1}[m]*(m - b +1) + P_{b-1}[m-1].

This recurrence resembles Stirling numbers? If no constraints (T_b=1 all b), dp_N[m] should be number of permutations of length N with max exactly m? But after N positions max must be N, so dp_N[N]=N!, others 0. Let's test recurrence no constraints:
dp_1[m]=1 for m>=1.
dp_2[m]=dp_1[m]*(m-1)+P_1[m-1].
For m=1: dp_1[1]*0 + P_1[0]=0. m=2: dp_1[2]*1 + P_1[1]=1+1=2. dp_2[2]=2. m=3: dp_1[3]*2 + P_1[2]=2+2=4? But after 2 positions from 3 values, number with max exactly3 is choose one of first two positions as3 and other <3: 2*2=4. yes. After N steps, sum should N!.

- All L values are unforced. At each step, choices = v.

The number of ways to choose the L positions (ordered by labels) such that the minimum of new positions is exactly s (s<r) or no new position <r can be counted.

A_j (with A_0=0, A_{m+1}=N maybe for trivial deadline N). All values in block j have deadline k_j (or N for last block if no constraint). Within a block, the threshold d_v is constant = k (or N for trivial). As argued, at most one forced step at the beginning of the block if current r > k. But now we can compute the count using a product formula maybe.

Let's process blocks from high values to low values. Let block j (for j=m down to1) consist of values L_j = A_{j-1}+1 .. A_j, where define A_0=0, A_{m+1}=N? Let's define blocks from top:
Block 0 (trivial): values A_m+1..N, deadline N, length L0 = N-A_m.
Block j (1..m): values A_{j-1}+1..A_j, deadline k_j, length L_j = A_j - A_{j-1}.
Processing order: Block 0 first (values N down to A_m+1), then Block m, Block m-1, ..., Block 1.

For a block with deadline k and length L, and current minimum r:
- If r <= k: all L steps unforced. The number of choices for the block is product of unused counts for those values. If the block values are v = A_{j-1}+1..A_j? Wait processing descending, the first value in block j is A_j, then A_j-1, ..., A_{j-1}+1. The unused counts at these steps are A_j, A_j-1, ..., A_{j-1}+1 (because before placing value v, used values >v count N-v, unused count v). So unforced block product = A_j! / A_{j-1}!.
- If r > k: the first step (value A_j) is forced with k choices instead of A_j choices. The remaining steps are unforced with counts A_j-1, ..., A_{j-1}+1. Product = k * (A_j-1)! / A_{j-1}!.
Thus the block multiplier is either U_j = A_j! / A_{j-1}! or F_j = k_j * (A_j-1)! / A_{j-1}! = (k_j / A_j) * U_j.

The total count is the sum over possible states r of products of these multipliers, where the state r after each block determines whether next block is forced. But maybe the state r after a block is always <= the block's deadline k_j if the block was forced or if previous r<=k_j. If r<=k_j at start, after unforced steps r may decrease but remains <=k_j. For the next block j-1, deadline k_{j-1} < k_j (since k_j increasing with j, so k_{j-1} smaller). The condition for next block being forced is r > k_{j-1}. Let's set blocks:

This is a two-state DP over blocks! Because the only information needed for the next block is whether r <= next deadline. Since deadlines decrease, if r <= k_{j-1} then good; else bad. The exact value of r within <=k_{j-1} doesn't matter for future? For subsequent even smaller deadlines, if r <= k_{j-1}, it may still be > k_{j-2}, so we will need to know if r <= k_{j-2}. Let's examine the update for a block. Suppose before block j (processing block j with deadline k_j, values A_{j-1}+1..A_j), we have a distribution of r over positions 1..N. The block choices depend on whether r > k_j. After the block, the new r' = min(r, positions chosen in block). We need the distribution of r' for future. Can we compute the total count and the new distribution in closed form?

Maybe because the block values are a contiguous range, and the number of choices in unforced/forced cases has a simple product, the distribution of r' after the block is uniform over some range? Let's test.

For an unforced block of length L with values v = a+1..A (where a=A_{j-1}, A=A_j), we choose an ordered injection of L values into unused positions. The current used set has minimum r. The number of ways to end with new minimum r' = s (where s <= min(r, maybe)) can be counted. But perhaps the total count of assignments after processing all blocks can be expressed as a product over blocks of (k_j - A_{j-1})? Let's test with examples.

Let's compute using the block product idea for examples.

Example r=[1,3,3]: constraints m=1, k1=1, A1=3. Blocks:
Block0 trivial: values 4..3 none? N=3, A1=3, L0=0.
Block1: values 1..3, deadline k1=1, length3.
Before block1, r=∞ >1, forced. Product F1 = k1 * (A1-1)! / A0! = 1 * 2! /1? A0=0, A0! =1, so F=2. Count2. Good.

r=[1,2,3]: constraints k1=1,A1=2; k2=2,A2=3.
Blocks:
Block0: values 4..3 none.
Block2: values A1+1..A2 = 3..3, deadline k2=2, length1. Before block2, r=∞ >2, forced. Product F2 = k2 * (A2-1)! / A1! = 2 * 2! / 2! =2? Wait A1=2, A1!=2, A2=3, (A2-1)!=2, F2=2*2/2=2. But if block2 has only value3, forced with k2=2 choices means σ3<=2 (2 choices). Good.
After block2, r = σ3 ∈ {1,2}. Next block1 deadline k1=1. We need know if r<=1. If σ3=1 (1 way), block1 unforced; if σ3=2 (1 way), block1 forced.
Block1: values 1..2, deadline1, length2.
- If unforced (r<=1): product U1 = A1! / A0! = 2!/1=2.
- If forced (r>1): product F1 = k1 * (A1-1)! / A0! =1*1!/1=1.
Total = 1*2 + 1*1 =3. Matches. So need distribution.

This is like a Markov chain with two states per block. We can maintain for each possible r? But maybe the distribution after a block is simple: after block2 with r=σ3, the count for r=1 is1, r=2 is1. Then block1 update.

For larger N, the distribution of r after a block may be over many values. But perhaps we can maintain a generating function where r contributes to future forced/unforced only via comparison to next deadline. Since deadlines are k_j, and k_j are increasing. The possible r values that matter are intervals (k_{j-1}, k_j] etc. There are O(m) intervals. We could maintain counts for each interval, and update with block formulas. m can be O(N), so O(N^2) if each block updates all intervals. But maybe each block only affects intervals <=k_j, and the number of intervals processed is total O(N) if we use prefix sums.

Let's derive the update for a block in terms of the distribution of r.

Let before block j (with parameters a=A_{j-1}, A=A_j, k=k_j), we have a count dp[r] for the minimum used position among values >A (i.e., already processed higher values). We process values A, A-1, ..., a+1.

Case 1: r > k (bad). Then the first value A is forced to be chosen in [1,k]. After that, the remaining L-1 values are unforced. The new minimum r' will be the minimum of the positions chosen in this block (since old r>k and all forced/unforced positions? Wait unforced later can choose positions >k or <k; old r>k. If later choose a position >k, it doesn't affect r' because old r>k and that position >k could be less than old r? Actually old r is the minimum used position among higher values, say r=5, k=3. Later unforced choices can choose position 4, which is >k but <old r, so r' would become 4. So r' is the minimum of old r and all new positions. Since old r>k, and forced first position <=k, r' is definitely <=k. But it could be the minimum of all new positions, including those >k but <old r. So r' is simply the minimum position among all positions chosen in this block (because forced first <=k < old r, so old r is not the minimum). Thus r' is the minimum of L distinct positions chosen in a certain constrained process: the first position (for value A) is restricted to [1,k], the remaining L-1 positions are any unused positions (but since old r>k, all positions <=k are unused; positions >k may have some used among higher values, but we are choosing from unused positions; the minimum of new positions is what matters). The count of ways resulting in r'=s can be computed.

Case 2: r <= k (good). Then all L values are unforced. The new minimum r' = min(r, min of new positions). Since old r<=k. The minimum of new positions could be <r or >=r. The distribution depends on old r.

This is complex but maybe manageable with combinatorics of choosing minimum.

However, maybe there is an even simpler product formula if we use the original constraints M_{k_j} >= A_j. Let's try to count permutations satisfying these m constraints directly using inclusion-exclusion or known "rook polynomial" on a Ferrers board that is a "staircase" and might factor.

The constraints R_{A_j} <= k_j for j=1..m. Since A_j and k_j are increasing. This is equivalent to: For each j, the first k_j positions contain at least one value >= A_j. Consider the positions of the values A_j. Maybe we can count by placing the "critical" values A_1, A_2, ..., A_m? Note A_j increasing, and k_j increasing. The condition for j is that among the first k_j positions, there is a value >=A_j. Since A_j increasing, if value A_j itself appears in first k_j, then condition holds (because A_j >= A_j). If a larger value appears, also. The constraints are nested: For j+1, need value >=A_{j+1} by k_{j+1} (later). This is like each critical value A_j has a deadline k_j by which it or a larger value must appear. Since larger values have later deadlines, the critical value A_j might be "covered" by a larger value appearing before k_j.

This is analogous to scheduling jobs with deadlines where each job can cover all smaller constraints. We can count by considering the first critical value that appears early.

Let's define the positions of values A_1, A_2, ..., A_m, N? Actually values >=A_j include A_j, A_{j+1}, ..., N. The earliest among these is R_{A_j}. The constraints R_{A_j} <= k_j. Since R_{A_j} is nondecreasing in j. We need an increasing sequence R_j = R_{A_j} with R_j <= k_j. Also R_j is the minimum position among values >=A_j. The values between A_j and A_{j+1}-1 (if any) have positions > R_j? Not necessarily.

Maybe count by the positions of the "suffix minima" of the set of critical values. The sequence R_{A_j} is determined by the minimum position among the values in the suffix. If we know the earliest position among values >=A_j, it is the minimum of:
- position of A_j
- R_{A_{j+1}} (since suffix [A_{j+1},N] subset)
Thus R_j = min(pos(A_j), R_{j+1}). This is exactly the suffix minimum process restricted to critical values plus maybe values >A_m. We can count by processing critical values from high to low, with blocks of non-critical values contributing factorial factors.

Maybe we can derive a product formula using the "block" process but with a small state if we aggregate over intervals between k_j. Let's attempt to compute the DP over intervals efficiently.

Let's define the distinct deadlines k_j. Since k_j are increasing. The possible minimum r after processing some blocks is always <= current deadline k_j (after block j). For the next block j-1, we only care if r <= k_{j-1} or not. Thus at each step, we can maintain two counts:
- G_j: number of ways after processing blocks 0..j such that r <= k_{j-1} (good for next block)
- B_j: number of ways such that r > k_{j-1} (bad for next block)
But to update from block j to j-1, we need to know the distribution of r within (k_{j-1}, k_j] vs <=k_{j-1}. The two-state G/B relative to k_{j-1} might be enough if we can compute the transition probabilities for the block from the current distribution relative to k_j? Wait before block j, we need know if r <= k_j to decide forced. We can maintain counts relative to k_j as well. Let's define before processing block j, we have counts:
G_j^before: r <= k_j (block unforced)
B_j^before: r > k_j (block forced)
After block j, we need counts relative to k_{j-1}:
G_{j-1}^after: r' <= k_{j-1}
B_{j-1}^after: r' > k_{j-1}
Can we compute these from (G_j^before, B_j^before) alone? The transition for r' depends on the actual r in the good case (r<=k_j), because if r is already <=k_{j-1}, then r'<=k_{j-1} automatically; if r in (k_{j-1}, k_j], then r' may become <=k_{j-1} if a new position <=k_{j-1} is chosen. So we need split G_j^before into r<=k_{j-1} and r in (k_{j-1}, k_j]. Thus we need counts for each interval between deadlines, not just binary.

However, the number of intervals is m, and we process m blocks. We could maintain an array of counts for intervals (k_{i-1}, k_i] and update with range operations. Since m up to N, O(m^2) worst. Need better.

Let's revisit the DP recurrence and see if we can optimize it using the fact that thresholds T_b are nondecreasing and only change at k_j. The recurrence:
dp_b[m] = dp_{b-1}[m]*(m-b+1) + P_{b-1}[m-1], for m >= T_b, else 0.
We need after K steps sum dp_K[m] (then multiply by (N-K)!).

This is a linear recurrence. Maybe we can compute dp_K for all m in O(N log N) using divide-and-conquer? N=2e5, O(N sqrt N) maybe acceptable? But Python needs O(N log N).

Let's see if the recurrence can be transformed to a known sequence. Let's compute dp_b[m] with thresholds. Suppose T_b is a step function. Between thresholds, T_b constant. If T_b = v, then states m<v are zero. For m>=v, recurrence same. The zeroing below v means we discard states with small maximum. This is equivalent to starting the no-constraint process at step b0 with initial dp only on m>=v.

Maybe we can use the closed form for no-constraint DP to jump over intervals. If for a range of steps T_b = v constant, and we know dp at start, the evolution is the same as the standard recurrence. The standard recurrence has an explicit solution:
Starting from any initial vector dp_{b0}[m], after L steps without constraints, the value at m is:
dp_{b0+L}[m] = ∑_{x} dp_{b0}[x] * (number of ways to choose L values from remaining to end with max m, given current max x).
This can be expressed combinatorially. If current max is x, and we place L additional distinct values, the final max m is max(x, max of new values). The number of ways to choose and order L values from the remaining N-(b0) values such that the maximum of new values is exactly y (or <=y) can be computed. This might allow interval jumps with range updates.

Let's derive the transition over L unconstrained steps.

Suppose before an interval of length L (positions b0+1 .. b0+L), we have used b0 values, current maximum x. We choose L new values from the remaining N-b0 values and order them. The number of resulting sequences with final maximum m is:
- If m < x: impossible because max stays at least x.
- If m = x: all L new values must be < x. Number of ways = P(x-1 - (used values < x), L)? But used values < x count? Since current maximum x, among the b0 used values, one is x, and the rest b0-1 are < x. So available values < x not yet used: (x-1) - (b0-1) = x - b0. We need choose L distinct values from these and order: P(x-b0, L) = (x-b0)!/(x-b0-L)! if L <= x-b0.
- If m > x: the maximum of new values is m, so m must be chosen among the L new values, and all other L-1 new values < m (and not used). The number of ways: choose position of m among L: L choices. For the other L-1 positions, choose/order from available values < m excluding used values. Available values < m not used: (m-1) - (b0-1) = m - b0 (since used values <m are all b0-1 old values plus? old max x<m, so all old values <m, count b0; wait used values <m = b0 (all old values), because x<m. Thus available <m = (m-1) - b0 = m - b0 -1? Let's carefully count.

At start of interval, used values count b0, all <= x < m. So used values < m = b0. Available values < m = (m-1) - b0 = m - b0 -1. We need choose L-1 values from these and order: P(m-b0-1, L-1). Then multiply by L for position of m. So count = L * P(m-b0-1, L-1) = L * (m-b0-1)! / (m-b0-L)!.
This matches no-constraint formula for starting from x=0,b0=0: L * (m-1)!/(m-L)!.

Thus over an interval of L unconstrained steps, the transition kernel from old max x to new max m is:
if m = x: A_x = P(x-b0, L) (if x>=b0+L? actually need x-b0 >= L)
if m > x: B_{x,m} = L * P(m-b0-1, L-1) for m > x.
Notice B does not depend on x! It only depends on m and b0,L. This is huge. The number of ways to end with new max m > x is independent of the old max x (as long as x<m). Because all old values are <m, so the available pool below m is the same regardless of x (since x<m, old values count b0 all below m). Thus the transition from any x<m to m is the same.

Therefore, if we have a distribution dp[x] at the start of an unconstrained interval, after L steps:
dp_new[m] = dp[m] * P(m-b0, L) + (∑_{x < m} dp[x]) * L * P(m-b0-1, L-1).
This is exactly the recurrence but with L steps aggregated! Let's verify for L=1: P(m-b0,1)=m-b0, and L*P(m-b0-1,0)=1, so dp_new[m]=dp[m]*(m-b0)+prefix_sum[m-1]. With b0 = b-1, this matches dp_b recurrence (m-b+1). Good.

This aggregated transition can be computed for all m in O(N) per interval if we have prefix sums and can compute the coefficients. If the number of intervals (distinct thresholds) is m, O(N*m) worst. But maybe we can process intervals and update only m>=threshold; still O(N^2) if many intervals. However, the transition coefficients depend on m and b0,L. We need faster.

But note the thresholds T_b are nondecreasing and only change at k_j. The intervals are exactly the gaps between constraint positions. The number of intervals m can be O(N). But perhaps the total length of intervals is K <= N, and we can use the fact that within an interval, T_b constant, so we can apply the aggregated transition once per interval instead of per step. That reduces O(N^2) to O(N * #intervals) if we update all m each interval. Still O(N^2) in worst case (e.g., alternating S gives r increasing by1 each step, #intervals=N, K=N). Need further optimization.

Let's derive from the block process and see if we can compute the final count using a simple recurrence over constraints only.

We have constraints (k_j, A_j) with k_j increasing, A_j increasing, A_j > k_j. Let's define K_j = k_j, A_j. We want count of permutations π of [N] such that for each j, M_{K_j} >= A_j.

Thus in each block, at most one forced step. The total count can be expressed as a sum over which blocks are forced. - If before r > k_j (forced): after block, r' is the minimum of positions chosen in block. The probability r' <= k_{j-1} can be computed from the block parameters, independent of r (since r>k_j and k_{j-1}<k_j, old r doesn't affect r'<=k_{j-1}? Actually old r > k_j > k_{j-1}, so r' <= k_{j-1} iff the minimum of new positions <= k_{j-1}. This depends only on block choices, not on old r. Good!
- If before r <= k_j (unforced): after block, r' = min(r, new_min). We need count r' <= k_{j-1}. This depends on whether old r <= k_{j-1} or old r in (k_{j-1}, k_j]. If old r <= k_{j-1}, then r' <= k_{j-1} automatically. If old r > k_{j-1}, then r' <= k_{j-1} iff new_min <= k_{j-1}. Thus we need split the unforced cases by old r <= k_{j-1} vs > k_{j-1}. But maybe we can maintain a cumulative distribution over r at the deadlines. Since there are m deadlines, we can maintain an array of counts for intervals (k_{i-1}, k_i] and update each block with range operations. The update might be expressible as:
- Forced case (r > k_j): adds to all r' <= k_j a distribution independent of old r, times total bad count.
- Unforced case (r <= k_j): old r distribution shifted/truncated; if old r <= k_{j-1}, it remains; if old r in (k_{j-1}, k_j], it survives only if new_min <= k_{j-1}, with a probability depending on old r? Thus the number of ways for new_min <= k_{j-1} is independent of old r (as long as old r > k_{j-1}). If old r <= k_{j-1}, automatic. Therefore, we only need the total count of old r <= k_{j-1} and old r > k_{j-1} within the unforced group! This is exactly two sub-states. Great.

Thus we can maintain for each block boundary the counts of r in intervals relative to the next deadline, but maybe a binary split per step is enough if we process blocks from high to low and maintain:

- B: count of r > current next deadline.
But before block j, the "current next deadline" is k_j? Let's set up a loop.

Let's define after processing blocks with deadline > k_j (i.e., blocks 0..j), we have counts:
G_j = number of partial assignments with r <= k_{j-1} (good for block j-1)

But to process block j (deadline k_j), we need before block j counts relative to k_j. That would be after processing blocks > k_j (i.e., blocks 0..j+1? Wait block j is values A_{j-1}+1..A_j, deadline k_j. Blocks with higher deadline are block 0 (trivial) and blocks m, m-1, ..., j+1. After processing those, we need counts relative to k_j to decide if block j forced. Let's call state before block j:
G_j^pre = count r <= k_j
B_j^pre = count r > k_j

We can compute G_{j-1}^pre from G_j^pre and B_j^pre plus the split of G_j^pre into r <= k_{j-1} and r in (k_{j-1}, k_j]. But if we only maintain G_j^pre (r<=k_j) and B_j^pre, we don't know the split. Thus we need an array C_i = count of r <= k_i after processing current higher blocks. Since k_i increasing, C_i is a cumulative distribution over the deadlines. This is starting to look like a DP with range updates that can be done with a Fenwick tree if we process blocks and update C_i for i<j by a formula depending on C_j and B_j.

Let's derive the update for C_i after processing block j.

Let before block j:
- Total count T = C_j + B_j, where C_j = count r <= k_j, B_j = count r > k_j.
- For i<j, C_i = count r <= k_i (known from previous updates).

Block j parameters: a = A_{j-1}, A = A_j, L = A - a, k = k_j. Values in block: A, A-1, ..., a+1. We process them.

Case 1: r > k (bad), count B_j.
The first value A is forced to choose a position in [1,k]. Then remaining L-1 values unforced. The new minimum r' is the minimum of the L positions chosen in this block (old r>k so ignored). We need update C_i for i<j: count of outcomes with r' <= k_i. Since k_i < k. This is the number of ways to choose positions for the block such that at least one chosen position <= k_i, times? Actually r' <= k_i iff the minimum of the L chosen positions <= k_i. Since all positions <=k are unused at the start (because r>k), and positions >k may have some used but irrelevant. The number of ordered assignments of the block (with first forced to <=k) that have min <= k_i. This depends on k_i, k, A, a. Let's call F_bad(i) for this block.

Case 2: r <= k (good), count C_j.
All L values unforced. New r' = min(r, new_min).
For i<j:
- If old r <= k_i, then r' <= k_i automatically. Count of such old r is C_i (known).
- If old r > k_i, then r' <= k_i iff new_min <= k_i. Since old r > k_i, positions <=k_i are unused. The number of ordered assignments of the block with min <= k_i is independent of old r (as long as old r > k_i). Let's call U_min(i) for the unforced block. Thus increment from C_j good cases = C_i * (total unforced block assignments) + (C_j - C_i) * U_min(i)? Wait C_j includes old r<=k_j. Among them, C_i have r<=k_i, and C_j - C_i have r in (k_i, k_j]. For the latter, need new_min <= k_i. So contribution = C_i * U_total + (C_j - C_i) * U_min(i), where U_total = A! / a! (unforced block product), U_min(i) = number of unforced block assignments with min <= k_i.

Additionally, from bad cases B_j: contribution = B_j * F_min(i), where F_min(i) = number of forced block assignments with min <= k_i.

Then new C_i' = C_i * U_total + (C_j - C_i) * U_min(i) + B_j * F_min(i).

This is a linear transformation on the array C_i for i<j. It involves C_i and C_j. If we process j from m down to1, we can update all i<j using this formula. Naively O(m^2). But maybe U_min(i) and F_min(i) have simple forms allowing range updates with prefix sums.

Given time, maybe we should implement the DP with O(N log N) using the recurrence and a segment tree? Let's see if the recurrence can be computed in O(N log N) with lazy propagation.

Recurrence per step b:
dp_b[m] = 0 for m < T_b.
For m >= T_b:
dp_b[m] = dp_{b-1}[m]*(m - b +1) + P_{b-1}[m-1].

We can maintain dp and prefix sums. The update for all m can be seen as:
dp_new[m] = a_m * dp_old[m] + prefix_old[m-1], where a_m = m - b +1.
This is a linear transformation. If we process b from 1 to N, T_b nondecreasing. We only need final sum after N, not all intermediate. Could use divide-and-conquer over b with polynomial operations? The recurrence is similar to computing the number of permutations with restricted positions, which can be done by evaluating a determinant of an N x N matrix in O(N log^2 N) maybe, but implementing in Python is hard.

Let's derive general process count using inclusion-exclusion over forced steps. For e_i nonincreasing, at step i, if current min m > e_i, forced. The current min m is the minimum of previous τ's. The total number of partial sequences of length i-1 is P(N, i-1) = N!/(N-i+1)!. The count with m <= e_i is total - P(N-e_i, i-1). At step i, if m<=e_i, choices = N-i+1; if m>e_i, choices = e_i (since all <=e_i unused). Thus the expected number of choices? We can count total sequences by summing over i? Maybe the total number of full permutations can be computed by a product of probabilities? Not independent.

But the process is Markov with state m. However, the count of sequences where at step i the min is > e_i is exactly the number of partial sequences using only values > e_i: P(N-e_i, i-1). This is independent of previous thresholds! Because if all previous values > e_i, then automatically m>e_i. Since e_i nonincreasing, if previous values > e_i, they also satisfy earlier thresholds? Earlier thresholds e_j >= e_i, so values >e_i are >e_j, so yes. Thus the set of partial sequences with m>e_i is simply all injective sequences of length i-1 from the N-e_i values >e_i. Count = P(N-e_i, i-1) = (N-e_i)!/(N-e_i-(i-1))!.

This is huge! We can compute the total count by a product of conditional probabilities? At step i, among all partial sequences of length i-1, those with m>e_i have count P(N-e_i, i-1). Those with m<=e_i have count P(N, i-1) - P(N-e_i, i-1). The number of choices at step i:
- If m<=e_i: N-i+1 choices.
- If m>e_i: e_i choices.
Thus the total number of partial sequences of length i (before applying future constraints) is:
Total_i = [P(N, i-1) - P(N-e_i, i-1)] * (N-i+1) + P(N-e_i, i-1) * e_i.
But is this equal to the actual count of valid partial sequences of length i satisfying constraints up to i? Since the condition at step i depends on m after choosing τ_i. We used before-choice m to determine allowed choices. The number of choices that satisfy prefix min <= e_i after step i is exactly as above. This should be the total number of valid partial sequences of length i, regardless of future. Let's test with e=[2,1,1,1], N=4.

Compute P(n,k)=n!/(n-k)!.
i=1: e1=2. P(N,0)=1, P(N-e1,0)=1. Total_1 = (1-1)*4 +1*2 =2. Valid τ1 <=2: 2 choices. Good.

Maybe we can maintain the distribution of the minimum m over valid partial sequences. The process for τ is:
At step i, if m <= e_i, choose any unused value; if m > e_i, forced to choose <=e_i. This is exactly the same as the original process but with min. The state m is the minimum used value (not position). The number of valid partial sequences with minimum m can be updated. This is symmetric to the dp we had, and maybe easier because e_i nonincreasing.

Let's define dp_i[m] = number of valid partial sequences of length i with current minimum exactly m. Initially dp_0[∞]=1? At step i:
- If m <= e_i: choose any unused value. The new minimum m' = min(m, new value). If choose new value < m, m' decreases; if >m, m' stays m. Number of choices for new value <m: m-1 - (used values <m). Used values <m count? Since current minimum m, all used values are >=m, and at least one equals m. Thus used values <m =0! Wait if current minimum is m, no used value <m. So all values <m are unused. Number of choices to set new minimum m'<m is 1 for each m'<m. Choices to keep m: choose any unused value >m. Number of unused values >m = N - m - (used values >m). Used values >m count = (i-1) - 1 = i-2 (since one value equals m). So choices keep m = N - m - (i-2) = N - m - i +2.
- If m > e_i: forced to choose a value <=e_i. Since m>e_i, all values <=e_i are unused. New minimum m' = chosen value (<=e_i). For each m'<=e_i, 1 choice. No keep.
This DP is similar but maybe simpler because used values <m is 0. Let's test.

This DP is much simpler! State m is the minimum used value. The update:
For each step i with threshold e_i:
If m <= e_i:
  dp_new[m] += dp[m] * (N - m - i + 2)  (keep m)
  for m' < m: dp_new[m'] += dp[m]  (choose m' as new min)
If m > e_i:
  for m' <= e_i: dp_new[m'] += dp[m]  (forced choose m')
Also if m <= e_i, choosing a value <m decreases min; choosing a value >m keeps min. There is no option to choose a value between? Values <m are all unused, yes.

This is similar to the original DP but with min and thresholds e_i nonincreasing. Can we optimize this? The transition:
- From m<=e_i: add dp[m] to all m'<m (range prefix add), and dp[m]*(N-m-i+2) to same m.
- From m>e_i: add dp[m] to all m'<=e_i (prefix add up to e_i).

Let's write the full update for all m':

dp_new[m'] =
- from keep: if m' <= e_i, dp[m'] * (N - m' - i + 2)
- from decrease: ∑_{m > m'} dp[m] for m <= e_i (since only m<=e_i can choose smaller m'<m)
- from forced: ∑_{m > e_i} dp[m] if m' <= e_i.

So for m' <= e_i:
dp_new[m'] = dp[m']*(N - m' - i + 2) + ∑_{m=m'+1}^{e_i} dp[m] + ∑_{m=e_i+1}^{N} dp[m]
= dp[m']*(N - m' - i + 2) + ∑_{m=m'+1}^{N} dp[m].
For m' > e_i:
dp_new[m'] = 0? Wait if m' > e_i, can it be the new minimum? If current m<=e_i, choosing a new value >m would keep m (not m'), so m' cannot be >e_i unless current m=m'>e_i, but then m>e_i forced to choose <=e_i, so no. Thus dp_new[m']=0 for m'>e_i. This makes sense: after step i, the minimum must be <=e_i (constraint satisfied). So the support of dp is always [1, e_i].

Thus the update is:
For all m' <= e_i:
dp_new[m'] = dp[m']*(N - m' - i + 2) + suffix_sum_dp[m'+1],
where suffix_sum_dp[m'+1] = ∑_{m=m'+1}^{N} dp[m] (old dp).
For m' > e_i: 0.

This is exactly analogous to the original recurrence but with suffix sums and min! And it's much simpler because the support shrinks to [1,e_i] each step. e_i is nonincreasing, so support shrinks.

For m' > e_i, set dp[m']=0.

- If m < r: value m must be among the L new values, and no value <m appears. Since old min r>m, no old value <m. Count = L * P(N-m-1 - (i0-1)? Wait available values >m excluding old used values. At this point, I'll implement the DP with suffix sums using a segment tree with lazy propagation for the operation \(dp[m] = a_m dp[m] + suffix_sum[m+1]\). This operation can be applied to all m in a range in O(log N) if we store the sum and use a "prefix/suffix sum" transformation? Let's think.

We can maintain dp in a segment tree over m=1..N. The operation on a range [1,e]:
For each m in [1,e], new[m] = a_m old[m] + S_old[m+1], where S_old[m+1] is the suffix sum of old dp from m+1 to N.
This is equivalent to:
new[m] = a_m old[m] + (TotalOld - PrefixOld[m]).
So new[m] = TotalOld + (a_m old[m] - PrefixOld[m]).
This depends on PrefixOld[m], the prefix sum up to m. If we could maintain prefix sums, applying this to all m is like adding TotalOld to all, subtracting PrefixOld[m], and scaling old[m]. The term PrefixOld[m] is a global function. This is not a simple lazy affine transform because PrefixOld[m] varies with m and changes globally.

However, if we process m from e down to1, we can compute suffix sum incrementally. The total cost is sum e. To reduce, we can use a sqrt decomposition: divide m into blocks. For each block, maintain dp values and suffix sums? The update for a block can be done in O(block size) if we rebuild, but maybe O(N sqrt N) too high for 2e5? 2e5*sqrt ~9e7 maybe borderline in Python.

=== ANSWER ===
>
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()

    # positions of black vertices
    q = []
    for i, ch in enumerate(S, 1):
        if ch == 'B':
            q.append(i)

    # r_i = first white index after the i-th black vertex
    r = [0] * (N + 1)  # 1-indexed
    for i, pos in enumerate(q, 1):
        r[i] = pos - i + 1

    # Necessary conditions
    if r[1] != 1 or r[N] > N:
        print(0)
        return

    # d_v = min b such that r_{b+1} >= v
    d = [0] * (N + 1)  # 1-indexed
    b = 1
    for v in range(1, N + 1):
        while b <= N - 1 and r[b + 1] < v:
            b += 1
        if b <= N - 1:
            d[v] = b
        else:
            d[v] = N

    # e_i = d_{N-i+1}
    e = [0] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        e[i] = d[N - i + 1]

    # DP over the reversed permutation.
    # dp[m] = number of ways after current prefix with current minimum exactly m.
    dp = [0] * (N + 2)

    # First step
    lim = e[1]
    for m in range(1, lim + 1):
        dp[m] = 1

    # Suffix sums of dp
    suffix = [0] * (N + 3)
    for i in range(2, N + 1):
        lim = e[i]
        if lim <= 0:
            print(0)
            return

        # compute suffix sums of old dp
        suffix[N + 1] = 0
        for m in range(N, 0, -1):
            suffix[m] = (suffix[m + 1] + dp[m]) % MOD

        newdp = [0] * (N + 2)
        for m in range(1, lim + 1):
            keep = dp[m] * (N - m - i + 2) % MOD
            add = suffix[m + 1]
            newdp[m] = (keep + add) % MOD

        dp = newdp

    ans = sum(dp) % MOD
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
